I know that probably sounds strange. Lost control? I don’t know… I don’t really think of it like that.

Like, sometimes on a job, everyone’s standing around eating from whatever got picked up—subs or donuts or those gas station muffins—and I’ll be thinking more about how long the primer needs to dry, or whether the client is going to complain about the tape line, and then food is just kind of there. But… no, I’m not sure I’ve had that “can’t stop eating” thing, if that’s what you mean.

Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, if I’m being honest.

It’s not like I’m sitting there making some whole chart or anything—well, sometimes I do get kind of stuck thinking about it while I’m working, like if we’re in an old house with those narrow hallways and you’re carrying paint trays and trying not to hit the trim. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I don’t really look at myself and think, like, this is some big emergency the way other people make it sound.

I know people keep saying I’m too thin or acting worried, but to me it feels more like I’m just… disciplined, I guess. And busy. When you’re working all day, climbing up and down, hauling drop cloths, cleaning brushes, driving between jobs—sometimes you just don’t eat a big meal like everyone thinks you should.

So yeah, I worry more about gaining and not moving right than I worry about being “too thin.” I don’t really see my weight as this crisis. It feels like people are making a big deal out of it.

And then I’m picturing trying to get around ladders and tight rooms and carrying stuff, and feeling like I can’t move right. So I guess if that worry wasn’t there, yeah, I might eat more.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control over how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Lost control? I don’t know… I don’t really think of it like that.\n\nLike, sometimes on a job, everyone’s standing around eating from whatever got picked up—subs or donuts or those gas station muffins—and I’ll be thinking more about how long the primer needs to dry, or whether the client is going to complain about the tape line, and then food is just kind of there. But… no, I’m not sure I’ve had that “can’t stop eating” thing, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI’m usually pretty aware of what I’m having. Maybe too aware. But losing control… I don’t know, that doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting what you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some whole chart or anything—well, sometimes I do get kind of stuck thinking about it while I’m working, like if we’re in an old house with those narrow hallways and you’re carrying paint trays and trying not to hit the trim. But if I eat something heavy, like pizza or one of those huge sandwiches people bring to a site, I get this panicky feeling after. Like I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to.\n\nSo… yeah. I guess being afraid of gaining weight is a big reason I limit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, or is your concern about gaining weight more about how it would affect your ability to work and move around?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really look at myself and think, like, this is some big emergency the way other people make it sound.\n\nI know people keep saying I’m too thin or acting worried, but to me it feels more like I’m just… disciplined, I guess. And busy. When you’re working all day, climbing up and down, hauling drop cloths, cleaning brushes, driving between jobs—sometimes you just don’t eat a big meal like everyone thinks you should.\n\nSo yeah, I worry more about gaining and not moving right than I worry about being “too thin.” I don’t really see my weight as this crisis. It feels like people are making a big deal out of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think I look… mostly how I should, I guess. I mean, maybe other people see something different, because they keep making comments, like it’s this huge emergency.

But I don’t really see it that way. To me it’s more like—when you’re working, you stay busy, you don’t sit around eating all day, and you get used to being careful. Like on a job, if I’m sanding or cutting in around windows, I’m not thinking, oh, my body shape is a problem. I’m thinking about not dripping paint on somebody’s floor.

So… no, I don’t really think my weight is some big crisis. I feel like people make too much out of it, like being disciplined and busy is suddenly a terrible thing.

I mean… I guess so? When I look in the mirror, I don’t see what everyone else seems to be seeing.

Yeah… I think I would. I mean, the mirror isn’t really the main thing.

It’s more like if I eat something heavy—like pizza, or one of those big sandwiches people bring to a job site—I start getting this panicky feeling, like I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Heavier. Slower. And then I’m thinking about climbing ladders or squeezing around rooms with paint trays and all that, and it just feels like I won’t move right.

Like, I know that sounds dramatic, but when you’re up on a ladder cutting in a ceiling line, even feeling a little off can mess with you. So even if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still be worried about that feeling.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess it’s… both, maybe? But honestly the physical part is what hits me first.

Like if I eat something heavy—pizza, or one of those big sandwiches someone brings to the job—I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Not just looking different, but bigger in a way where I feel slower, like I won’t move right. And then I start thinking about ladders, or squeezing around a room with drop cloths and paint trays, and it’s like… what if I can’t do that the way I need to?

I mean, I know how that sounds. But it’s not just standing in front of a mirror thinking about looks. It’s that feeling of being heavier and not being able to move around the way I’m used to.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… well, my clinic doctor kind of pushed me to come in. She’s been on me about how I eat, saying it’s not enough, I guess. I mean, most days I just keep it simple—coffee in the morning, and then if I’m between jobs I might grab a few bites of something. I do painting and paperhanging, so sometimes you’re up on ladders or trying not to get paste everywhere, and stopping for food is just… inconvenient. Then later I’ll have a small dinner if I’m not buried in class paperwork. So… I guess that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying food or looking forward to eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t really think about food that way, like looking forward to it. Sometimes if someone brings in something nice to a job site I’ll notice it smells good, but then you’re trying to finish a wall before the light changes, and with old houses the trim is never straight, so you end up fussing with it way longer than you planned. So… I guess eating is more just something that happens when there’s time, not something I’m waiting for.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? My work pants are definitely hanging pretty loose now, like I keep having to pull them up, which is annoying when you’re climbing around or carrying drop cloths. And people on job sites keep saying I look too thin, which—honestly, people comment on everything at those places, the paint color, the weather, who parked where…\n\nBut I don’t really see it as some big thing. I’ve just leaned out, I think. I’m on my feet all day, up and down ladders, moving around. So… I guess there’s been some weight off, yeah, but I don’t know that I’d call it significant.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds kind of dramatic. But if I eat something heavy—like pizza, or one of those big sandwiches the guys bring to a site—I get this panicky feeling after, like I’m going to wake up bigger or heavy or slowed down.\n\nAnd with my work, you’re squeezing around rooms and going up ladders and carrying trays, and if you feel bulky or sluggish it just… it makes the whole day harder. I know that probably sounds strange. I just don’t want to feel like I can’t move the way I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Probably, yeah. I mean… if I didn’t get that feeling afterward, I think I’d be less careful about it.\n\nIt’s more like, if I eat something heavy—pizza or one of those big sandwiches people bring to a job—I start thinking I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow. And then I’m picturing trying to get around ladders and tight rooms and carrying stuff, and feeling like I can’t move right. So I guess if that worry wasn’t there, yeah, I might eat more.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control over how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Lost control? I don’t know… I don’t really think of it like that.\n\nLike, sometimes on a job, everyone’s standing around eating from whatever got picked up—subs or donuts or those gas station muffins—and I’ll be thinking more about how long the primer needs to dry, or whether the client is going to complain about the tape line, and then food is just kind of there. But… no, I’m not sure I’ve had that “can’t stop eating” thing, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI’m usually pretty aware of what I’m having. Maybe too aware. But losing control… I don’t know, that doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting what you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some whole chart or anything—well, sometimes I do get kind of stuck thinking about it while I’m working, like if we’re in an old house with those narrow hallways and you’re carrying paint trays and trying not to hit the trim. But if I eat something heavy, like pizza or one of those huge sandwiches people bring to a site, I get this panicky feeling after. Like I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to.\n\nSo… yeah. I guess being afraid of gaining weight is a big reason I limit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, or is your concern about gaining weight more about how it would affect your ability to work and move around?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really look at myself and think, like, this is some big emergency the way other people make it sound.\n\nI know people keep saying I’m too thin or acting worried, but to me it feels more like I’m just… disciplined, I guess. And busy. When you’re working all day, climbing up and down, hauling drop cloths, cleaning brushes, driving between jobs—sometimes you just don’t eat a big meal like everyone thinks you should.\n\nSo yeah, I worry more about gaining and not moving right than I worry about being “too thin.” I don’t really see my weight as this crisis. It feels like people are making a big deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the way you should, in terms of weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: I think I look… mostly how I should, I guess. I mean, maybe other people see something different, because they keep making comments, like it’s this huge emergency.\n\nBut I don’t really see it that way. To me it’s more like—when you’re working, you stay busy, you don’t sit around eating all day, and you get used to being careful. Like on a job, if I’m sanding or cutting in around windows, I’m not thinking, oh, my body shape is a problem. I’m thinking about not dripping paint on somebody’s floor.\n\nSo… no, I don’t really think my weight is some big crisis. I feel like people make too much out of it, like being disciplined and busy is suddenly a terrible thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess so? When I look in the mirror, I don’t see what everyone else seems to be seeing.\n\nLike, people act like my weight is this emergency, and I just don’t… I don’t see it that way. I look and I think, okay, I’m busy, I work a physical job, I’m careful about what I eat. That doesn’t seem so shocking to me.\n\nIt’s like when I’m on a job and someone makes a big thing out of a tiny mark on the wall before the second coat is even on—sometimes people just overreact before they understand the whole picture. So yeah, I think what I see matches me more than what other people are saying. They make it sound way worse than it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, the mirror isn’t really the main thing.\n\nIt’s more like if I eat something heavy—like pizza, or one of those big sandwiches people bring to a job site—I start getting this panicky feeling, like I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Heavier. Slower. And then I’m thinking about climbing ladders or squeezing around rooms with paint trays and all that, and it just feels like I won’t move right.\n\nLike, I know that sounds dramatic, but when you’re up on a ladder cutting in a ceiling line, even feeling a little off can mess with you. So even if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still be worried about that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely about how you would feel physically?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… both, maybe? But honestly the physical part is what hits me first.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy—pizza, or one of those big sandwiches someone brings to the job—I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Not just looking different, but bigger in a way where I feel slower, like I won’t move right. And then I start thinking about ladders, or squeezing around a room with drop cloths and paint trays, and it’s like… what if I can’t do that the way I need to?\n\nI mean, I know how that sounds. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… well, my clinic doctor kind of pushed me to come in. She’s been on me about how I eat, saying it’s not enough, I guess. I mean, most days I just keep it simple—coffee in the morning, and then if I’m between jobs I might grab a few bites of something. I do painting and paperhanging, so sometimes you’re up on ladders or trying not to get paste everywhere, and stopping for food is just… inconvenient. Then later I’ll have a small dinner if I’m not buried in class paperwork. So… I guess that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying food or looking forward to eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t really think about food that way, like looking forward to it. Sometimes if someone brings in something nice to a job site I’ll notice it smells good, but then you’re trying to finish a wall before the light changes, and with old houses the trim is never straight, so you end up fussing with it way longer than you planned. So… I guess eating is more just something that happens when there’s time, not something I’m waiting for.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? My work pants are definitely hanging pretty loose now, like I keep having to pull them up, which is annoying when you’re climbing around or carrying drop cloths. And people on job sites keep saying I look too thin, which—honestly, people comment on everything at those places, the paint color, the weather, who parked where…\n\nBut I don’t really see it as some big thing. I’ve just leaned out, I think. I’m on my feet all day, up and down ladders, moving around. So… I guess there’s been some weight off, yeah, but I don’t know that I’d call it significant.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds kind of dramatic. But if I eat something heavy—like pizza, or one of those big sandwiches the guys bring to a site—I get this panicky feeling after, like I’m going to wake up bigger or heavy or slowed down.\n\nAnd with my work, you’re squeezing around rooms and going up ladders and carrying trays, and if you feel bulky or sluggish it just… it makes the whole day harder. I know that probably sounds strange. I just don’t want to feel like I can’t move the way I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Probably, yeah. I mean… if I didn’t get that feeling afterward, I think I’d be less careful about it.\n\nIt’s more like, if I eat something heavy—pizza or one of those big sandwiches people bring to a job—I start thinking I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow. And then I’m picturing trying to get around ladders and tight rooms and carrying stuff, and feeling like I can’t move right. So I guess if that worry wasn’t there, yeah, I might eat more.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control over how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Lost control? I don’t know… I don’t really think of it like that.\n\nLike, sometimes on a job, everyone’s standing around eating from whatever got picked up—subs or donuts or those gas station muffins—and I’ll be thinking more about how long the primer needs to dry, or whether the client is going to complain about the tape line, and then food is just kind of there. But… no, I’m not sure I’ve had that “can’t stop eating” thing, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI’m usually pretty aware of what I’m having. Maybe too aware. But losing control… I don’t know, that doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting what you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some whole chart or anything—well, sometimes I do get kind of stuck thinking about it while I’m working, like if we’re in an old house with those narrow hallways and you’re carrying paint trays and trying not to hit the trim. But if I eat something heavy, like pizza or one of those huge sandwiches people bring to a site, I get this panicky feeling after. Like I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to.\n\nSo… yeah. I guess being afraid of gaining weight is a big reason I limit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, or is your concern about gaining weight more about how it would affect your ability to work and move around?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really look at myself and think, like, this is some big emergency the way other people make it sound.\n\nI know people keep saying I’m too thin or acting worried, but to me it feels more like I’m just… disciplined, I guess. And busy. When you’re working all day, climbing up and down, hauling drop cloths, cleaning brushes, driving between jobs—sometimes you just don’t eat a big meal like everyone thinks you should.\n\nSo yeah, I worry more about gaining and not moving right than I worry about being “too thin.” I don’t really see my weight as this crisis. It feels like people are making a big deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the way you should, in terms of weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: I think I look… mostly how I should, I guess. I mean, maybe other people see something different, because they keep making comments, like it’s this huge emergency.\n\nBut I don’t really see it that way. To me it’s more like—when you’re working, you stay busy, you don’t sit around eating all day, and you get used to being careful. Like on a job, if I’m sanding or cutting in around windows, I’m not thinking, oh, my body shape is a problem. I’m thinking about not dripping paint on somebody’s floor.\n\nSo… no, I don’t really think my weight is some big crisis. I feel like people make too much out of it, like being disciplined and busy is suddenly a terrible thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess so? When I look in the mirror, I don’t see what everyone else seems to be seeing.\n\nLike, people act like my weight is this emergency, and I just don’t… I don’t see it that way. I look and I think, okay, I’m busy, I work a physical job, I’m careful about what I eat. That doesn’t seem so shocking to me.\n\nIt’s like when I’m on a job and someone makes a big thing out of a tiny mark on the wall before the second coat is even on—sometimes people just overreact before they understand the whole picture. So yeah, I think what I see matches me more than what other people are saying. Heavier. Slower. And then I’m thinking about climbing ladders or squeezing around rooms with paint trays and all that, and it just feels like I won’t move right.\n\nLike, I know that sounds dramatic, but when you’re up on a ladder cutting in a ceiling line, even feeling a little off can mess with you. So even if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still be worried about that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely about how you would feel physically?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… both, maybe? I guess being afraid of gaining weight is a big reason I limit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, or is your concern about gaining weight more about how it would affect your ability to work and move around?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really look at myself and think, like, this is some big emergency the way other people make it sound.\n\nI know people keep saying I’m too thin or acting worried, but to me it feels more like I’m just… disciplined, I guess. And busy. When you’re working all day, climbing up and down, hauling drop cloths, cleaning brushes, driving between jobs—sometimes you just don’t eat a big meal like everyone thinks you should.\n\nSo yeah, I worry more about gaining and not moving right than I worry about being “too thin.” I don’t really see my weight as this crisis. It feels like people are making a big deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the way you should, in terms of weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: I think I look… mostly how I should, I guess. I mean, maybe other people see something different, because they keep making comments, like it’s this huge emergency.\n\nBut I don’t really see it that way. To me it’s more like—when you’re working, you stay busy, you don’t sit around eating all day, and you get used to being careful. Like on a job, if I’m sanding or cutting in around windows, I’m not thinking, oh, my body shape is a problem. I’m thinking about not dripping paint on somebody’s floor.\n\nSo… no, I don’t really think my weight is some big crisis. I feel like people make too much out of it, like being disciplined and busy is suddenly a terrible thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess so? When I look in the mirror, I don’t see what everyone else seems to be seeing.\n\nLike, people act like my weight is this emergency, and I just don’t… I don’t see it that way. I look and I think, okay, I’m busy, I work a physical job, I’m careful about what I eat. That doesn’t seem so shocking to me.\n\nIt’s like when I’m on a job and someone makes a big thing out of a tiny mark on the wall before the second coat is even on—sometimes people just overreact before they understand the whole picture. So yeah, I think what I see matches me more than what other people are saying. They make it sound way worse than it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, the mirror isn’t really the main thing.\n\nIt’s more like if I eat something heavy—like pizza, or one of those big sandwiches people bring to a job site—I start getting this panicky feeling, like I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Heavier. Slower. And then I’m thinking about climbing ladders or squeezing around rooms with paint trays and all that, and it just feels like I won’t move right.\n\nLike, I know that sounds dramatic, but when you’re up on a ladder cutting in a ceiling line, even feeling a little off can mess with you. So even if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still be worried about that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely about how you would feel physically?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… both, maybe? But honestly the physical part is what hits me first.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy—pizza, or one of those big sandwiches someone brings to the job—I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Not just looking different, but bigger in a way where I feel slower, like I won’t move right. And then I start thinking about ladders, or squeezing around a room with drop cloths and paint trays, and it’s like… what if I can’t do that the way I need to?\n\nI mean, I know how that sounds. But it’s not just standing in front of a mirror thinking about looks. It’s that feeling of being heavier and not being able to move around the way I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how it would affect your appearance or how it would impact your ability to work and perform physical activities?\n\nPatient: More the work part, I think. I mean, appearance is in there somewhere, I’m not saying I don’t think about it, but what scares me is feeling like I’d be… bigger and slower.\n\nLike if I eat pizza or one of those big sandwiches, I start thinking, what if I wake up and I can’t move the same? And then I picture being on a ladder or trying to get around a room with all the drop cloths and paint cans everywhere, and I just feel panicky, like I won’t be able to do my job right.\n\nI know it probably sounds kind of all over the place, but that’s where my mind goes. Not just how I look, but how I’d move.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. So… I guess there’s been some weight off, yeah, but I don’t know that I’d call it significant.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds kind of dramatic. But if I eat something heavy—like pizza, or one of those big sandwiches the guys bring to a site—I get this panicky feeling after, like I’m going to wake up bigger or heavy or slowed down.\n\nAnd with my work, you’re squeezing around rooms and going up ladders and carrying trays, and if you feel bulky or sluggish it just… it makes the whole day harder. I know that probably sounds strange. I just don’t want to feel like I can’t move the way I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Probably, yeah. I mean… if I didn’t get that feeling afterward, I think I’d be less careful about it.\n\nIt’s more like, if I eat something heavy—pizza or one of those big sandwiches people bring to a job—I start thinking I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow. And then I’m picturing trying to get around ladders and tight rooms and carrying stuff, and feeling like I can’t move right. So I guess if that worry wasn’t there, yeah, I might eat more.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control over how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Lost control? I don’t know… I don’t really think of it like that.\n\nLike, sometimes on a job, everyone’s standing around eating from whatever got picked up—subs or donuts or those gas station muffins—and I’ll be thinking more about how long the primer needs to dry, or whether the client is going to complain about the tape line, and then food is just kind of there. But losing control… I don’t know, that doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting what you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some whole chart or anything—well, sometimes I do get kind of stuck thinking about it while I’m working, like if we’re in an old house with those narrow hallways and you’re carrying paint trays and trying not to hit the trim. But if I eat something heavy, like pizza or one of those huge sandwiches people bring to a site, I get this panicky feeling after. Like I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to.\n\nSo… yeah. I guess being afraid of gaining weight is a big reason I limit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, or is your concern about gaining weight more about how it would affect your ability to work and move around?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really look at myself and think, like, this is some big emergency the way other people make it sound.\n\nI know people keep saying I’m too thin or acting worried, but to me it feels more like I’m just… disciplined, I guess. And busy. When you’re working all day, climbing up and down, hauling drop cloths, cleaning brushes, driving between jobs—sometimes you just don’t eat a big meal like everyone thinks you should.\n\nSo yeah, I worry more about gaining and not moving right than I worry about being “too thin.” I don’t really see my weight as this crisis. It feels like people are making a big deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the way you should, in terms of weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: I think I look… mostly how I should, I guess. I mean, maybe other people see something different, because they keep making comments, like it’s this huge emergency.\n\nBut I don’t really see it that way. To me it’s more like—when you’re working, you stay busy, you don’t sit around eating all day, and you get used to being careful. Like on a job, if I’m sanding or cutting in around windows, I’m not thinking, oh, my body shape is a problem. I’m thinking about not dripping paint on somebody’s floor.\n\nSo… no, I don’t really think my weight is some big crisis. I feel like people make too much out of it, like being disciplined and busy is suddenly a terrible thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess so? When I look in the mirror, I don’t see what everyone else seems to be seeing.\n\nLike, people act like my weight is this emergency, and I just don’t… I don’t see it that way. I look and I think, okay, I’m busy, I work a physical job, I’m careful about what I eat. That doesn’t seem so shocking to me.\n\nIt’s like when I’m on a job and someone makes a big thing out of a tiny mark on the wall before the second coat is even on—sometimes people just overreact before they understand the whole picture. So yeah, I think what I see matches me more than what other people are saying. They make it sound way worse than it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, the mirror isn’t really the main thing.\n\nIt’s more like if I eat something heavy—like pizza, or one of those big sandwiches people bring to a job site—I start getting this panicky feeling, like I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. And then I’m thinking about climbing ladders or squeezing around rooms with paint trays and all that, and it just feels like I won’t move right.\n\nLike, I know that sounds dramatic, but when you’re up on a ladder cutting in a ceiling line, even feeling a little off can mess with you. Not just looking different, but bigger in a way where I feel slower, like I won’t move right. And then I start thinking about ladders, or squeezing around a room with drop cloths and paint trays, and it’s like… what if I can’t do that the way I need to?\n\nI mean, I know how that sounds. But it’s not just standing in front of a mirror thinking about looks. It’s that feeling of being heavier and not being able to move around the way I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how it would affect your appearance or how it would impact your ability to work and perform physical activities?\n\nPatient: More the work part, I think. I mean, appearance is in there somewhere, I’m not saying I don’t think about it, but what scares me is feeling like I’d be… bigger and slower.\n\nLike if I eat pizza or one of those big sandwiches, I start thinking, what if I wake up and I can’t move the same? And then I picture being on a ladder or trying to get around a room with all the drop cloths and paint cans everywhere, and I just feel panicky, like I won’t be able to do my job right.\n\nI know it probably sounds kind of all over the place, but that’s where my mind goes. Not just how I look, but how I’d move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but that’s not really the main thing my brain grabs onto.\n\nIt’s more like if I eat something heavy, like pizza or one of those huge sandwiches guys bring to a job site, I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Like heavier and slower. When I look in the mirror, I don’t see what everyone else seems to be seeing.\n\nLike, people act like my weight is this emergency, and I just don’t… I don’t see it that way. I look and I think, okay, I’m busy, I work a physical job, I’m careful about what I eat. That doesn’t seem so shocking to me.\n\nIt’s like when I’m on a job and someone makes a big thing out of a tiny mark on the wall before the second coat is even on—sometimes people just overreact before they understand the whole picture. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… well, my clinic doctor kind of pushed me to come in. She’s been on me about how I eat, saying it’s not enough, I guess. I mean, most days I just keep it simple—coffee in the morning, and then if I’m between jobs I might grab a few bites of something. I do painting and paperhanging, so sometimes you’re up on ladders or trying not to get paste everywhere, and stopping for food is just… inconvenient. Then later I’ll have a small dinner if I’m not buried in class paperwork. So… I guess that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying food or looking forward to eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t really think about food that way, like looking forward to it. Sometimes if someone brings in something nice to a job site I’ll notice it smells good, but then you’re trying to finish a wall before the light changes, and with old houses the trim is never straight, so you end up fussing with it way longer than you planned. So… I guess eating is more just something that happens when there’s time, not something I’m waiting for.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? My work pants are definitely hanging pretty loose now, like I keep having to pull them up, which is annoying when you’re climbing around or carrying drop cloths. And people on job sites keep saying I look too thin, which—honestly, people comment on everything at those places, the paint color, the weather, who parked where…\n\nBut I don’t really see it as some big thing. I’ve just leaned out, I think. I’m on my feet all day, up and down ladders, moving around. So… I guess there’s been some weight off, yeah, but I don’t know that I’d call it significant.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds kind of dramatic. But if I eat something heavy—like pizza, or one of those big sandwiches the guys bring to a site—I get this panicky feeling after, like I’m going to wake up bigger or heavy or slowed down.\n\nAnd with my work, you’re squeezing around rooms and going up ladders and carrying trays, and if you feel bulky or sluggish it just… it makes the whole day harder. I know that probably sounds strange. I just don’t want to feel like I can’t move the way I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Probably, yeah. I mean… if I didn’t get that feeling afterward, I think I’d be less careful about it.\n\nIt’s more like, if I eat something heavy—pizza or one of those big sandwiches people bring to a job—I start thinking I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow. And then I’m picturing trying to get around ladders and tight rooms and carrying stuff, and feeling like I can’t move right. So I guess if that worry wasn’t there, yeah, I might eat more.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control over how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Lost control? I don’t know… I don’t really think of it like that.\n\nLike, sometimes on a job, everyone’s standing around eating from whatever got picked up—subs or donuts or those gas station muffins—and I’ll be thinking more about how long the primer needs to dry, or whether the client is going to complain about the tape line, and then food is just kind of there. But… no, I’m not sure I’ve had that “can’t stop eating” thing, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI’m usually pretty aware of what I’m having. Maybe too aware. But losing control… I don’t know, that doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting what you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some whole chart or anything—well, sometimes I do get kind of stuck thinking about it while I’m working, like if we’re in an old house with those narrow hallways and you’re carrying paint trays and trying not to hit the trim. But if I eat something heavy, like pizza or one of those huge sandwiches people bring to a site, I get this panicky feeling after. Like I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to.\n\nSo… yeah. I guess being afraid of gaining weight is a big reason I limit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, or is your concern about gaining weight more about how it would affect your ability to work and move around?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really look at myself and think, like, this is some big emergency the way other people make it sound.\n\nI know people keep saying I’m too thin or acting worried, but to me it feels more like I’m just… disciplined, I guess. And busy. When you’re working all day, climbing up and down, hauling drop cloths, cleaning brushes, driving between jobs—sometimes you just don’t eat a big meal like everyone thinks you should.\n\nSo yeah, I worry more about gaining and not moving right than I worry about being “too thin.” I don’t really see my weight as this crisis. It feels like people are making a big deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the way you should, in terms of weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: I think I look… mostly how I should, I guess. I mean, maybe other people see something different, because they keep making comments, like it’s this huge emergency.\n\nBut I don’t really see it that way. To me it’s more like—when you’re working, you stay busy, you don’t sit around eating all day, and you get used to being careful. Like on a job, if I’m sanding or cutting in around windows, I’m not thinking, oh, my body shape is a problem. I’m thinking about not dripping paint on somebody’s floor.\n\nSo… no, I don’t really think my weight is some big crisis. I feel like people make too much out of it, like being disciplined and busy is suddenly a terrible thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess so? When I look in the mirror, I don’t see what everyone else seems to be seeing.\n\nLike, people act like my weight is this emergency, and I just don’t… I don’t see it that way. I look and I think, okay, I’m busy, I work a physical job, I’m careful about what I eat. That doesn’t seem so shocking to me.\n\nIt’s like when I’m on a job and someone makes a big thing out of a tiny mark on the wall before the second coat is even on—sometimes people just overreact before they understand the whole picture. So yeah, I think what I see matches me more than what other people are saying. They make it sound way worse than it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, the mirror isn’t really the main thing.\n\nIt’s more like if I eat something heavy—like pizza, or one of those big sandwiches people bring to a job site—I start getting this panicky feeling, like I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Heavier. Slower. And then I’m thinking about climbing ladders or squeezing around rooms with paint trays and all that, and it just feels like I won’t move right.\n\nLike, I know that sounds dramatic, but when you’re up on a ladder cutting in a ceiling line, even feeling a little off can mess with you. So even if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still be worried about that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely about how you would feel physically?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… both, maybe? But honestly the physical part is what hits me first.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy—pizza, or one of those big sandwiches someone brings to the job—I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Not just looking different, but bigger in a way where I feel slower, like I won’t move right. And then I start thinking about ladders, or squeezing around a room with drop cloths and paint trays, and it’s like… what if I can’t do that the way I need to?\n\nI mean, I know how that sounds. But it’s not just standing in front of a mirror thinking about looks. It’s that feeling of being heavier and not being able to move around the way I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how it would affect your appearance or how it would impact your ability to work and perform physical activities?\n\nPatient: More the work part, I think. I mean, appearance is in there somewhere, I’m not saying I don’t think about it, but what scares me is feeling like I’d be… bigger and slower.\n\nLike if I eat pizza or one of those big sandwiches, I start thinking, what if I wake up and I can’t move the same? And then I picture being on a ladder or trying to get around a room with all the drop cloths and paint cans everywhere, and I just feel panicky, like I won’t be able to do my job right.\n\nI know it probably sounds kind of all over the place, but that’s where my mind goes. Not just how I look, but how I’d move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but that’s not really the main thing my brain grabs onto.\n\nIt’s more like if I eat something heavy, like pizza or one of those huge sandwiches guys bring to a job site, I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Like heavier and slower. And then I start picturing being on a ladder, or trying to move around a room with paint cans and drop cloths everywhere, and I’m thinking, what if I can’t move the way I need to?\n\nI know you asked about looking unattractive, and I’m not saying I never think about that, but mostly it’s that feeling of being bigger and not being able to work right.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly because you think it would make it harder for you to do your job physically?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yeah. That’s the part that scares me the most.\n\nLike, if I eat something heavy—pizza or one of those big sandwiches, the kind that just sits there in your stomach—I get this panicky thought that I’m going to wake up bigger and slower. And then I start picturing myself on a ladder, or trying to get around a room with all the paint stuff and drop cloths everywhere, and I’m like, what if I can’t move the way I need to?\n\nI know that sounds kind of dramatic, but it feels really real in the moment. It’s mostly about not being able to do the physical part of the job right.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how your body would look or how it would affect your physical abilities?\n\nPatient: It’s more about the physical part, I think. I mean, I’m aware of how I look too, but that’s not what hits me first.\n\nIt’s like… if I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panic that I’m going to wake up bigger and slower. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, maybe, but it’s not really like I’m picturing a shape in the mirror and thinking, “I’d hate that.” It’s more… if I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger and slower.

And then my mind goes straight to work, like being up on ladders or moving around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans everywhere. I start thinking I won’t be able to move the way I need to. So it’s not just the body shape thing, it’s more what being bigger would mean for how I can move.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… well, my clinic doctor kind of pushed me to come in. She’s been on me about how I eat, saying it’s not enough, I guess. I mean, most days I just keep it simple—coffee in the morning, and then if I’m between jobs I might grab a few bites of something. I do painting and paperhanging, so sometimes you’re up on ladders or trying not to get paste everywhere, and stopping for food is just… inconvenient. Then later I’ll have a small dinner if I’m not buried in class paperwork. So… I guess that’s why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying food or looking forward to eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I don’t really think about food that way, like looking forward to it. Sometimes if someone brings in something nice to a job site I’ll notice it smells good, but then you’re trying to finish a wall before the light changes, and with old houses the trim is never straight, so you end up fussing with it way longer than you planned. So… I guess eating is more just something that happens when there’s time, not something I’m waiting for.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? My work pants are definitely hanging pretty loose now, like I keep having to pull them up, which is annoying when you’re climbing around or carrying drop cloths. And people on job sites keep saying I look too thin, which—honestly, people comment on everything at those places, the paint color, the weather, who parked where…\n\nBut I don’t really see it as some big thing. I’ve just leaned out, I think. I’m on my feet all day, up and down ladders, moving around. So… I guess there’s been some weight off, yeah, but I don’t know that I’d call it significant.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds kind of dramatic. But if I eat something heavy—like pizza, or one of those big sandwiches the guys bring to a site—I get this panicky feeling after, like I’m going to wake up bigger or heavy or slowed down.\n\nAnd with my work, you’re squeezing around rooms and going up ladders and carrying trays, and if you feel bulky or sluggish it just… it makes the whole day harder. I know that probably sounds strange. I just don’t want to feel like I can’t move the way I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Probably, yeah. I mean… if I didn’t get that feeling afterward, I think I’d be less careful about it.\n\nIt’s more like, if I eat something heavy—pizza or one of those big sandwiches people bring to a job—I start thinking I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower somehow. And then I’m picturing trying to get around ladders and tight rooms and carrying stuff, and feeling like I can’t move right. So I guess if that worry wasn’t there, yeah, I might eat more.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control over how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Lost control? I don’t know… I don’t really think of it like that.\n\nLike, sometimes on a job, everyone’s standing around eating from whatever got picked up—subs or donuts or those gas station muffins—and I’ll be thinking more about how long the primer needs to dry, or whether the client is going to complain about the tape line, and then food is just kind of there. But… no, I’m not sure I’ve had that “can’t stop eating” thing, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI’m usually pretty aware of what I’m having. Maybe too aware. But losing control… I don’t know, that doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting what you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some whole chart or anything—well, sometimes I do get kind of stuck thinking about it while I’m working, like if we’re in an old house with those narrow hallways and you’re carrying paint trays and trying not to hit the trim. But if I eat something heavy, like pizza or one of those huge sandwiches people bring to a site, I get this panicky feeling after. Like I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to.\n\nSo… yeah. I guess being afraid of gaining weight is a big reason I limit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, or is your concern about gaining weight more about how it would affect your ability to work and move around?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really look at myself and think, like, this is some big emergency the way other people make it sound.\n\nI know people keep saying I’m too thin or acting worried, but to me it feels more like I’m just… disciplined, I guess. And busy. When you’re working all day, climbing up and down, hauling drop cloths, cleaning brushes, driving between jobs—sometimes you just don’t eat a big meal like everyone thinks you should.\n\nSo yeah, I worry more about gaining and not moving right than I worry about being “too thin.” I don’t really see my weight as this crisis. It feels like people are making a big deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the way you should, in terms of weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: I think I look… mostly how I should, I guess. I mean, maybe other people see something different, because they keep making comments, like it’s this huge emergency.\n\nBut I don’t really see it that way. To me it’s more like—when you’re working, you stay busy, you don’t sit around eating all day, and you get used to being careful. Like on a job, if I’m sanding or cutting in around windows, I’m not thinking, oh, my body shape is a problem. I’m thinking about not dripping paint on somebody’s floor.\n\nSo… no, I don’t really think my weight is some big crisis. I feel like people make too much out of it, like being disciplined and busy is suddenly a terrible thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess so? When I look in the mirror, I don’t see what everyone else seems to be seeing.\n\nLike, people act like my weight is this emergency, and I just don’t… I don’t see it that way. I look and I think, okay, I’m busy, I work a physical job, I’m careful about what I eat. That doesn’t seem so shocking to me.\n\nIt’s like when I’m on a job and someone makes a big thing out of a tiny mark on the wall before the second coat is even on—sometimes people just overreact before they understand the whole picture. So yeah, I think what I see matches me more than what other people are saying. They make it sound way worse than it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, the mirror isn’t really the main thing.\n\nIt’s more like if I eat something heavy—like pizza, or one of those big sandwiches people bring to a job site—I start getting this panicky feeling, like I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Heavier. Slower. And then I’m thinking about climbing ladders or squeezing around rooms with paint trays and all that, and it just feels like I won’t move right.\n\nLike, I know that sounds dramatic, but when you’re up on a ladder cutting in a ceiling line, even feeling a little off can mess with you. So even if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still be worried about that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely about how you would feel physically?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… both, maybe? But honestly the physical part is what hits me first.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy—pizza, or one of those big sandwiches someone brings to the job—I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Not just looking different, but bigger in a way where I feel slower, like I won’t move right. And then I start thinking about ladders, or squeezing around a room with drop cloths and paint trays, and it’s like… what if I can’t do that the way I need to?\n\nI mean, I know how that sounds. But it’s not just standing in front of a mirror thinking about looks. It’s that feeling of being heavier and not being able to move around the way I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how it would affect your appearance or how it would impact your ability to work and perform physical activities?\n\nPatient: More the work part, I think. I mean, appearance is in there somewhere, I’m not saying I don’t think about it, but what scares me is feeling like I’d be… bigger and slower.\n\nLike if I eat pizza or one of those big sandwiches, I start thinking, what if I wake up and I can’t move the same? And then I picture being on a ladder or trying to get around a room with all the drop cloths and paint cans everywhere, and I just feel panicky, like I won’t be able to do my job right.\n\nI know it probably sounds kind of all over the place, but that’s where my mind goes. Not just how I look, but how I’d move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but that’s not really the main thing my brain grabs onto.\n\nIt’s more like if I eat something heavy, like pizza or one of those huge sandwiches guys bring to a job site, I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Like heavier and slower. And then I start picturing being on a ladder, or trying to move around a room with paint cans and drop cloths everywhere, and I’m thinking, what if I can’t move the way I need to?\n\nI know you asked about looking unattractive, and I’m not saying I never think about that, but mostly it’s that feeling of being bigger and not being able to work right.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly because you think it would make it harder for you to do your job physically?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yeah. That’s the part that scares me the most.\n\nLike, if I eat something heavy—pizza or one of those big sandwiches, the kind that just sits there in your stomach—I get this panicky thought that I’m going to wake up bigger and slower. But losing control… I don’t know, that doesn’t sound like me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting what you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not like I’m sitting there making some whole chart or anything—well, sometimes I do get kind of stuck thinking about it while I’m working, like if we’re in an old house with those narrow hallways and you’re carrying paint trays and trying not to hit the trim. But if I eat something heavy, like pizza or one of those huge sandwiches people bring to a site, I get this panicky feeling after. Like I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to.\n\nSo… yeah. I guess being afraid of gaining weight is a big reason I limit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, or is your concern about gaining weight more about how it would affect your ability to work and move around?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really look at myself and think, like, this is some big emergency the way other people make it sound.\n\nI know people keep saying I’m too thin or acting worried, but to me it feels more like I’m just… disciplined, I guess. And busy. When you’re working all day, climbing up and down, hauling drop cloths, cleaning brushes, driving between jobs—sometimes you just don’t eat a big meal like everyone thinks you should.\n\nSo yeah, I worry more about gaining and not moving right than I worry about being “too thin.” I don’t really see my weight as this crisis. It feels like people are making a big deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the way you should, in terms of weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: I think I look… mostly how I should, I guess. I mean, maybe other people see something different, because they keep making comments, like it’s this huge emergency.\n\nBut I don’t really see it that way. To me it’s more like—when you’re working, you stay busy, you don’t sit around eating all day, and you get used to being careful. Like on a job, if I’m sanding or cutting in around windows, I’m not thinking, oh, my body shape is a problem. I’m thinking about not dripping paint on somebody’s floor.\n\nSo… no, I don’t really think my weight is some big crisis. I feel like people make too much out of it, like being disciplined and busy is suddenly a terrible thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess so? When I look in the mirror, I don’t see what everyone else seems to be seeing.\n\nLike, people act like my weight is this emergency, and I just don’t… I don’t see it that way. I look and I think, okay, I’m busy, I work a physical job, I’m careful about what I eat. That doesn’t seem so shocking to me.\n\nIt’s like when I’m on a job and someone makes a big thing out of a tiny mark on the wall before the second coat is even on—sometimes people just overreact before they understand the whole picture. So yeah, I think what I see matches me more than what other people are saying. They make it sound way worse than it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, the mirror isn’t really the main thing.\n\nIt’s more like if I eat something heavy—like pizza, or one of those big sandwiches people bring to a job site—I start getting this panicky feeling, like I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Heavier. Slower. And then I’m thinking about climbing ladders or squeezing around rooms with paint trays and all that, and it just feels like I won’t move right.\n\nLike, I know that sounds dramatic, but when you’re up on a ladder cutting in a ceiling line, even feeling a little off can mess with you. So even if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still be worried about that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely about how you would feel physically?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… both, maybe? But honestly the physical part is what hits me first.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy—pizza, or one of those big sandwiches someone brings to the job—I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Not just looking different, but bigger in a way where I feel slower, like I won’t move right. And then I start thinking about ladders, or squeezing around a room with drop cloths and paint trays, and it’s like… what if I can’t do that the way I need to?\n\nI mean, I know how that sounds. But it’s not just standing in front of a mirror thinking about looks. It’s that feeling of being heavier and not being able to move around the way I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how it would affect your appearance or how it would impact your ability to work and perform physical activities?\n\nPatient: More the work part, I think. I mean, appearance is in there somewhere, I’m not saying I don’t think about it, but what scares me is feeling like I’d be… bigger and slower.\n\nLike if I eat pizza or one of those big sandwiches, I start thinking, what if I wake up and I can’t move the same? And then I picture being on a ladder or trying to get around a room with all the drop cloths and paint cans everywhere, and I just feel panicky, like I won’t be able to do my job right.\n\nI know it probably sounds kind of all over the place, but that’s where my mind goes. Not just how I look, but how I’d move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but that’s not really the main thing my brain grabs onto.\n\nIt’s more like if I eat something heavy, like pizza or one of those huge sandwiches guys bring to a job site, I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Like heavier and slower. And then I start picturing being on a ladder, or trying to move around a room with paint cans and drop cloths everywhere, and I’m thinking, what if I can’t move the way I need to?\n\nI know you asked about looking unattractive, and I’m not saying I never think about that, but mostly it’s that feeling of being bigger and not being able to work right.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly because you think it would make it harder for you to do your job physically?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yeah. That’s the part that scares me the most.\n\nLike, if I eat something heavy—pizza or one of those big sandwiches, the kind that just sits there in your stomach—I get this panicky thought that I’m going to wake up bigger and slower. And then I start picturing myself on a ladder, or trying to get around a room with all the paint stuff and drop cloths everywhere, and I’m like, what if I can’t move the way I need to?\n\nI know that sounds kind of dramatic, but it feels really real in the moment. It’s mostly about not being able to do the physical part of the job right.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how your body would look or how it would affect your physical abilities?\n\nPatient: It’s more about the physical part, I think. I mean, I’m aware of how I look too, but that’s not what hits me first.\n\nIt’s like… if I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panic that I’m going to wake up bigger and slower. And then I start thinking about work, like being on ladders or squeezing around rooms with paint cans everywhere, and I worry I won’t be able to move the way I need to.\n\nSorry, I know I keep coming back to the ladders thing, but that’s really where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight because you believe it would change your body shape in a way that you wouldn't like?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it’s not really like I’m picturing a shape in the mirror and thinking, “I’d hate that.” It’s more… if I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger and slower.\n\nAnd then my mind goes straight to work, like being up on ladders or moving around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans everywhere. I start thinking I won’t be able to move the way I need to. So it’s not just the body shape thing, it’s more what being bigger would mean for how I can move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still limit your food intake if your job didn't require so much physical activity?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe? Like most days it’s just coffee in the morning, maybe a few bites of something between jobs, and then a small dinner later if I’m not too buried in class paperwork.\n\nThe paperwork for that class is honestly more annoying than the actual class, which sounds backwards, but… yeah. Yeah… I mean, I guess that’s part of it, but it’s also just become this routine where I keep food really simple. Like coffee in the morning, then if I’m between jobs I might grab a few bites of something, and then maybe a small dinner later if I’m not too busy.

So… I guess eating is more just something that happens when there’s time, not something I’m waiting for.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe? I’ve just leaned out, I think. I’m on my feet all day, up and down ladders, moving around. I know that probably sounds strange. I just don’t want to feel like I can’t move the way I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat more if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Probably, yeah. And then I’m picturing trying to get around ladders and tight rooms and carrying stuff, and feeling like I can’t move right. So I guess if that worry wasn’t there, yeah, I might eat more.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control over how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Lost control? But… no, I’m not sure I’ve had that “can’t stop eating” thing, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI’m usually pretty aware of what I’m having. Maybe too aware. But if I eat something heavy, like pizza or one of those huge sandwiches people bring to a site, I get this panicky feeling after. Like I’m going to wake up bigger, or slower, and then I won’t be able to move around ladders and rooms the way I need to.\n\nSo… yeah. I guess being afraid of gaining weight is a big reason I limit it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look too thin, or is your concern about gaining weight more about how it would affect your ability to work and move around?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t really look at myself and think, like, this is some big emergency the way other people make it sound.\n\nI know people keep saying I’m too thin or acting worried, but to me it feels more like I’m just… disciplined, I guess. And busy. When you’re working all day, climbing up and down, hauling drop cloths, cleaning brushes, driving between jobs—sometimes you just don’t eat a big meal like everyone thinks you should.\n\nSo yeah, I worry more about gaining and not moving right than I worry about being “too thin.” I don’t really see my weight as this crisis. It feels like people are making a big deal out of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look the way you should, in terms of weight and body shape?\n\nPatient: I think I look… mostly how I should, I guess. I mean, maybe other people see something different, because they keep making comments, like it’s this huge emergency.\n\nBut I don’t really see it that way. To me it’s more like—when you’re working, you stay busy, you don’t sit around eating all day, and you get used to being careful. Like on a job, if I’m sanding or cutting in around windows, I’m not thinking, oh, my body shape is a problem. I’m thinking about not dripping paint on somebody’s floor.\n\nSo… no, I don’t really think my weight is some big crisis. I feel like people make too much out of it, like being disciplined and busy is suddenly a terrible thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is the same as what you see in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess so? When I look in the mirror, I don’t see what everyone else seems to be seeing.\n\nLike, people act like my weight is this emergency, and I just don’t… I don’t see it that way. I look and I think, okay, I’m busy, I work a physical job, I’m careful about what I eat. That doesn’t seem so shocking to me.\n\nIt’s like when I’m on a job and someone makes a big thing out of a tiny mark on the wall before the second coat is even on—sometimes people just overreact before they understand the whole picture. So yeah, I think what I see matches me more than what other people are saying. They make it sound way worse than it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, the mirror isn’t really the main thing.\n\nIt’s more like if I eat something heavy—like pizza, or one of those big sandwiches people bring to a job site—I start getting this panicky feeling, like I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Heavier. Slower. And then I’m thinking about climbing ladders or squeezing around rooms with paint trays and all that, and it just feels like I won’t move right.\n\nLike, I know that sounds dramatic, but when you’re up on a ladder cutting in a ceiling line, even feeling a little off can mess with you. So even if I couldn’t see myself, I’d still be worried about that feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because of how you think you would look, or is it solely about how you would feel physically?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s… both, maybe? But honestly the physical part is what hits me first.\n\nLike if I eat something heavy—pizza, or one of those big sandwiches someone brings to the job—I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Not just looking different, but bigger in a way where I feel slower, like I won’t move right. And then I start thinking about ladders, or squeezing around a room with drop cloths and paint trays, and it’s like… what if I can’t do that the way I need to?\n\nI mean, I know how that sounds. But it’s not just standing in front of a mirror thinking about looks. It’s that feeling of being heavier and not being able to move around the way I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how it would affect your appearance or how it would impact your ability to work and perform physical activities?\n\nPatient: More the work part, I think. I mean, appearance is in there somewhere, I’m not saying I don’t think about it, but what scares me is feeling like I’d be… bigger and slower.\n\nLike if I eat pizza or one of those big sandwiches, I start thinking, what if I wake up and I can’t move the same? And then I picture being on a ladder or trying to get around a room with all the drop cloths and paint cans everywhere, and I just feel panicky, like I won’t be able to do my job right.\n\nI know it probably sounds kind of all over the place, but that’s where my mind goes. Not just how I look, but how I’d move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight is because you believe you would look unattractive if you gained weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… maybe a little, but that’s not really the main thing my brain grabs onto.\n\nIt’s more like if I eat something heavy, like pizza or one of those huge sandwiches guys bring to a job site, I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger somehow. Like heavier and slower. And then I start picturing being on a ladder, or trying to move around a room with paint cans and drop cloths everywhere, and I’m thinking, what if I can’t move the way I need to?\n\nI know you asked about looking unattractive, and I’m not saying I never think about that, but mostly it’s that feeling of being bigger and not being able to work right.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight mostly because you think it would make it harder for you to do your job physically?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mostly, yeah. That’s the part that scares me the most.\n\nLike, if I eat something heavy—pizza or one of those big sandwiches, the kind that just sits there in your stomach—I get this panicky thought that I’m going to wake up bigger and slower. And then I start picturing myself on a ladder, or trying to get around a room with all the paint stuff and drop cloths everywhere, and I’m like, what if I can’t move the way I need to?\n\nI know that sounds kind of dramatic, but it feels really real in the moment. It’s mostly about not being able to do the physical part of the job right.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight more about how your body would look or how it would affect your physical abilities?\n\nPatient: It’s more about the physical part, I think. I mean, I’m aware of how I look too, but that’s not what hits me first.\n\nIt’s like… if I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panic that I’m going to wake up bigger and slower. And then I start thinking about work, like being on ladders or squeezing around rooms with paint cans everywhere, and I worry I won’t be able to move the way I need to.\n\nSorry, I know I keep coming back to the ladders thing, but that’s really where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight because you believe it would change your body shape in a way that you wouldn't like?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe, but it’s not really like I’m picturing a shape in the mirror and thinking, “I’d hate that.” It’s more… if I eat something heavy, like pizza or a big sandwich, I get this panicky feeling that I’m going to wake up bigger and slower.\n\nAnd then my mind goes straight to work, like being up on ladders or moving around rooms with drop cloths and paint cans everywhere. I start thinking I won’t be able to move the way I need to. So it’s not just the body shape thing, it’s more what being bigger would mean for how I can move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still limit your food intake if your job didn't require so much physical activity?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe?
